<a id='question-141'></a>

### Question 141: Skin Pathology: Focus 03-41 (granulomatous diseases and streptococcal infection)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the ability to differentiate between granulomatous skin conditions, specifically focusing on the characteristic histopathological features and clinical context of erythema nodosum versus granuloma annulare, and the role of streptococcal infections in triggering certain skin manifestations. (本题考察区分肉芽肿性皮肤疾病的能力，特别是红斑结节病与花状肉芽肿的特征性组织病理学表现和临床背景，以及链球菌感染在触发某些皮肤表现中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old female presents to the dermatology clinic with a 2-week history of painful, erythematous nodules primarily located on the anterior shins. She reports a recent history of pharyngitis treated with antibiotics one month prior. Physical examination reveals multiple, tender, deep-seated, erythematous nodules ranging from 1 to 5 cm in diameter, with overlying skin appearing smooth and slightly warm. There is no associated fever, joint pain, or systemic symptoms. A skin biopsy is performed on one of the nodules. Histopathological examination reveals a dense infiltrate of neutrophils within the dermis, accompanied by lymphocytes and histiocytes. Notably, there are no caseating granulomas, giant cells, or vasculitis. Special stains for acid-fast bacilli are negative. The patient's recent pharyngitis history is confirmed.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism contributing to the patient's skin lesions?

(A) Immune complex deposition leading to leukocytoclastic vasculitis.
(B) Delayed-type hypersensitivity reaction to a persistent intracellular pathogen.
(C) Arthus reaction involving IgE-mediated mast cell degranulation.
(D) Complement-mediated endothelial injury secondary to streptococcal antigen deposition.
(E) Type III hypersensitivity reaction triggered by streptococcal antigens.

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 28-year-old female.
- **Chief Complaint:** Painful, erythematous nodules on anterior shins for 2 weeks.
- **Pertinent History:** Recent pharyngitis treated with antibiotics one month prior.
- **Physical Exam:** Tender, deep-seated, erythematous nodules (1-5 cm) on anterior shins, smooth overlying skin, warm to touch. No fever, joint pain, or systemic symptoms.
- **Histopathology:** Dense dermal infiltrate of neutrophils, lymphocytes, and histiocytes. No caseating granulomas, giant cells, or vasculitis. Negative AFB stains.
- **Clinical Picture Synthesis:** The presentation (painful, erythematous nodules on shins) following a recent pharyngitis strongly suggests erythema nodosum (EN). The histopathology (neutrophilic infiltrate without granulomas or vasculitis) is consistent with EN. The preceding streptococcal infection is a known trigger for EN.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most likely underlying mechanism*.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Erythema Nodosum (EN):** EN is a panniculitis (inflammation of subcutaneous fat) characterized by non-caseating granulomas (though sometimes predominantly neutrophilic, especially early on) in the septa of subcutaneous fat. Clinically, it presents as tender, red nodules, most commonly on the anterior shins.
- **Triggers:** EN is often triggered by underlying systemic conditions or infections, including streptococcal infections (especially pharyngitis), tuberculosis, sarcoidosis, inflammatory bowel disease, certain medications (e.g., oral contraceptives, sulfonamides), and malignancy.
- **Mechanism:** The exact pathogenesis is not fully understood, but it is widely considered a delayed-type hypersensitivity (Type IV) reaction. In the context of streptococcal infection, it is thought to be a Type III hypersensitivity reaction. Streptococcal antigens (e.g., streptolysin O, DNase B) can form immune complexes with antibodies. These immune complexes deposit in the small blood vessels of the dermis and subcutaneous fat, activating complement and recruiting neutrophils. This leads to inflammation and the characteristic panniculitis. While often described as Type IV, the association with preceding infections like strep throat, where immune complexes play a role, points towards a Type III component being significant, especially in this context. The histopathology showing neutrophils supports an immune complex-mediated inflammatory response.
- **Differential Diagnosis & Mechanisms:**
    - **Granuloma Annulare:** Presents as annular or arcuate, non-tender papules or plaques, often on extremities. Histology shows palisading histiocytes and necrobiosis, *not* neutrophils or granulomas. Mechanism is less clear, possibly Type IV hypersensitivity or related to altered lipid metabolism.
    - **Vasculitis (e.g., Leukocytoclastic Vasculitis):** Presents as palpable purpura, often on lower extremities. Histology shows fibrinoid necrosis of vessel walls and neutrophilic infiltrate *within* the vessel walls. Mechanism involves immune complex deposition (Type III) or anti-neutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies (ANCA-associated vasculitis). The vignette specifically states *no vasculitis* on histology.
    - **Sarcoidosis:** Can cause EN, but also presents with other skin lesions (papules, plaques) and systemic involvement (lungs, lymph nodes). Histology shows non-caseating granulomas with giant cells. Mechanism is Type IV hypersensitivity.
    - **Tuberculosis:** Can cause EN. Histology may show caseating granulomas (though not always present in skin lesions). Mechanism is Type IV hypersensitivity.
    - **Deep Fungal Infections:** Can cause granulomatous inflammation. Histology shows granulomas, often with specific fungal organisms visible on special stains. Mechanism is Type IV hypersensitivity.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Immune complex deposition leading to leukocytoclastic vasculitis:** While immune complex deposition is involved in Type III hypersensitivity, the vignette explicitly states *no vasculitis* was seen on histopathology. Leukocytoclastic vasculitis involves inflammation *within* the vessel walls, which was absent here. This option describes a different condition.
- (B) **Delayed-type hypersensitivity reaction to a persistent intracellular pathogen:** This describes a Type IV hypersensitivity reaction, often seen in conditions like tuberculosis or sarcoidosis, which can cause granulomatous inflammation. However, the histopathology in this case showed a neutrophilic infiltrate without granulomas, and the clinical context points towards a post-streptococcal reaction, which is more commonly associated with Type III mechanisms. While EN *can* be Type IV, the specific context here (recent strep throat, neutrophilic infiltrate) makes Type III more likely.
- (C) **Arthus reaction involving IgE-mediated mast cell degranulation:** An Arthus reaction is a localized Type III hypersensitivity reaction characterized by edema, hemorrhage, and necrosis, typically occurring hours after intradermal injection of an antigen in a previously sensitized individual. It involves immune complex deposition and complement activation, but it is not the primary mechanism for EN, and it is mediated by IgG and complement, not IgE and mast cells (which are characteristic of Type I hypersensitivity).
- (D) **Complement-mediated endothelial injury secondary to streptococcal antigen deposition:** This describes a component of Type III hypersensitivity (immune complex deposition activating complement). However, option (E) is more comprehensive as it explicitly names the *type* of hypersensitivity reaction (Type III) triggered by streptococcal antigens, which is the most widely accepted mechanism for EN following streptococcal infection. While complement activation is part of the process, stating the overall reaction type is more accurate.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Erythema Nodosum (EN)                                  | Granuloma Annulare (GA)                               | Leukocytoclastic Vasculitis (LCV)                      | Sarcoidosis                                           |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|
| **Clinical**         | Painful, red nodules, shins                            | Annular/arcuate, non-tender papules/plaques, extremities | Palpable purpura, often lower extremities              | Variable skin lesions, systemic symptoms common        |
| **Histology**        | Panniculitis, neutrophils, lymphocytes, histiocytes (often non-caseating granulomas) | Palisading histiocytes, necrobiosis                     | Fibrinoid necrosis of vessel walls, neutrophilic infiltrate | Non-caseating granulomas, giant cells                 |
| **Trigger**          | Strep infection, TB, sarcoidosis, IBD, drugs           | Often idiopathic, diabetes, hyperlipidemia             | Infections, drugs, autoimmune diseases, vasculitis      | Unknown, possibly infectious/genetic/environmental    |
| **Mechanism**        | Type III (immune complexes) or Type IV (delayed hypersensitivity) | Type IV (delayed hypersensitivity) or metabolic        | Type III (immune complexes) or ANCA-associated         | Type IV (delayed hypersensitivity)                    |
| **Key Finding**      | Tender nodules, recent strep infection                  | Annular morphology, non-tender                       | Palpable purpura, vessel wall inflammation             | Non-caseating granulomas, systemic involvement        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Erythema nodosum is a panniculitis often triggered by infections (like streptococcus) or systemic diseases. The most likely mechanism in the context of a recent streptococcal infection is a Type III hypersensitivity reaction involving immune complex deposition. Key differentials include granuloma annulare (non-tender, palisading histiocytes), vasculitis (palpable purpura, vessel wall inflammation), and other granulomatous diseases like sarcoidosis (systemic symptoms, non-caseating granulomas). Remember to correlate clinical presentation, histopathology, and potential triggers to arrive at the correct diagnosis and mechanism.

<a id='question-142'></a>

### Question 142: Skin Pathology: Focus 03-42 (Psoriasis is an autoimmune disorder with a clear g)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology of psoriasis, specifically the role of T-cell activation, cytokine release (TNF-α, IL-17, IL-23), and the resulting epidermal hyperplasia and inflammation. It also assesses the ability to differentiate psoriasis from other dermatological conditions based on clinical presentation and histological features.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to the dermatology clinic with a chronic, relapsing skin condition. He reports itchy, well-demarcated, erythematous plaques covered with silvery scales, primarily affecting his extensor surfaces (elbows, knees), scalp, and lower back. The lesions began several years ago and worsen during periods of stress. He denies any recent infections or medication changes. Physical examination reveals the characteristic plaques described above. A skin biopsy is performed from an active lesion. Histopathology shows marked epidermal acanthosis (thickening), parakeratosis (retention of nuclei in the stratum corneum), Munro's microabscesses (collections of neutrophils in the stratum corneum), and dilated, tortuous capillaries in the dermal papillae. Immunohistochemical staining reveals a significant increase in CD3+ T cells and CD117+ mast cells within the dermis and epidermis. Which of the following cellular processes is MOST directly responsible for the epidermal hyperplasia observed in this patient?

(A) Increased keratinocyte proliferation driven by epidermal growth factor (EGF) receptor activation.
(B) Decreased keratinocyte apoptosis mediated by Fas ligand (FasL) downregulation.
(C) Enhanced keratinocyte proliferation stimulated by cytokines released from activated T lymphocytes.
(D) Reduced epidermal turnover rate due to impaired differentiation of keratinocytes.
(E) Increased melanin production by melanocytes leading to hyperpigmentation and epidermal thickening.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male with a chronic, relapsing skin condition.
*   **Symptoms:** Itchy, well-demarcated, erythematous plaques with silvery scales.
*   **Location:** Extensor surfaces (elbows, knees), scalp, lower back – classic distribution for psoriasis.
*   **Triggers:** Worsens with stress – common trigger for psoriasis flares.
*   **Physical Exam:** Confirms characteristic plaques.
*   **Histopathology:**
    *   Epidermal acanthosis (thickening) - Key feature of psoriasis.
    *   Parakeratosis (retained nuclei in stratum corneum) - Key feature of psoriasis.
    *   Munro's microabscesses (neutrophils in stratum corneum) - Pathognomonic for psoriasis.
    *   Dilated, tortuous capillaries in dermal papillae - Contributes to erythema.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** Increased CD3+ T cells (T lymphocytes) and CD117+ mast cells - Indicates immune involvement.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST direct cause of epidermal hyperplasia (acanthosis).

The clinical presentation (plaques, scales, location, triggers) and histopathology (acanthosis, parakeratosis, Munro's microabscesses) are classic for psoriasis. The increased T cells point to an immune-mediated process. The question asks for the mechanism driving the epidermal hyperplasia (acanthosis). Psoriasis is known to be driven by T-cell activation, leading to the release of pro-inflammatory cytokines like TNF-α, IL-17, and IL-23. These cytokines directly stimulate keratinocyte proliferation, leading to acanthosis.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Psoriasis Pathophysiology:** Psoriasis is a chronic inflammatory skin disease characterized by epidermal hyperplasia (acanthosis), parakeratosis, and inflammation. It is considered an immune-mediated disease involving T cells (primarily Th1 and Th17 cells), dendritic cells, and keratinocytes.
*   **T-cell Activation:** In genetically susceptible individuals, environmental triggers (e.g., infection, stress, trauma) activate dendritic cells, which then present antigens to T cells.
*   **Cytokine Release:** Activated T cells migrate to the epidermis and release pro-inflammatory cytokines, including TNF-α, IL-17, and IL-23.
*   **Keratinocyte Proliferation:** These cytokines bind to receptors on keratinocytes, stimulating their proliferation and reducing their differentiation time. This leads to the characteristic epidermal thickening (acanthosis) and incomplete maturation (parakeratosis).
*   **Inflammation:** Cytokines also recruit neutrophils and other inflammatory cells to the epidermis, forming Munro's microabscesses and contributing to the inflammatory response.
*   **Vascular Changes:** Cytokines also induce angiogenesis and vasodilation, leading to the dilated capillaries seen in the dermal papillae.
*   **Epidermal Hyperplasia Mechanism:** The primary driver of epidermal hyperplasia in psoriasis is the stimulation of keratinocyte proliferation by cytokines released from activated T lymphocytes (Th1 and Th17 cells) infiltrating the epidermis and dermis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Increased keratinocyte proliferation driven by epidermal growth factor (EGF) receptor activation.** While EGF and its receptor (EGFR) are involved in normal keratinocyte proliferation and wound healing, they are not the primary drivers of the excessive proliferation seen in psoriasis. Psoriasis is primarily driven by inflammatory cytokines, not growth factors like EGF. EGFR activation might play a minor role, but it's not the *most direct* cause.
- (B): **Decreased keratinocyte apoptosis mediated by Fas ligand (FasL) downregulation.** FasL is a death receptor ligand that induces apoptosis. While alterations in apoptosis pathways can contribute to skin diseases, the primary mechanism in psoriasis is *increased* proliferation, not decreased apoptosis. In fact, some studies suggest increased FasL expression in psoriatic lesions, potentially leading to increased apoptosis, but this doesn't explain the overall hyperplasia. The dominant feature is hyperproliferation.
- (D): **Reduced epidermal turnover rate due to impaired differentiation of keratinocytes.** Psoriasis is characterized by an *accelerated* epidermal turnover rate, not reduced. The incomplete differentiation (leading to parakeratosis) is a consequence of the rapid proliferation stimulated by cytokines, not the primary cause of hyperplasia itself. The turnover rate is significantly faster than normal (e.g., 3-4 days vs. 28 days).
- (E): **Increased melanin production by melanocytes leading to hyperpigmentation and epidermal thickening.** While some psoriatic lesions can show post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation, increased melanin production is not the primary cause of the epidermal thickening (acanthosis). Acanthosis is due to keratinocyte proliferation, not melanocyte activity.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Psoriasis                                     | Option A (EGF-driven)                  | Option B (Decreased Apoptosis)          | Option D (Reduced Turnover)             | Option E (Melanin)                     |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Driver**      | T-cell mediated cytokine release (IL-17, TNFα) | EGF/EGFR signaling                      | FasL downregulation                   | Impaired differentiation              | Melanocyte activity                    |
| **Epidermal Change**    | Acanthosis (Hyperplasia), Parakeratosis       | Hyperplasia                             | Normal/Reduced Apoptosis               | Thinning/Normal Thickness              | Hyperpigmentation (not thickening)     |
| **Turnover Rate**       | Accelerated                                   | Normal/Accelerated                      | Normal                                | Reduced                                | Normal                                |
| **Key Cells Involved**  | T cells, Keratinocytes, Dendritic Cells       | Keratinocytes, Fibroblasts               | Keratinocytes                          | Keratinocytes                          | Melanocytes                            |
| **Histology**           | Acanthosis, Parakeratosis, Munro's abscesses  | Variable, often normal or hyperplasia   | Variable, apoptosis markers altered     | Variable, often thinning               | Melanin granules in keratinocytes      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Psoriasis is an immune-mediated inflammatory disease where activated T cells release cytokines (IL-17, TNF-α, IL-23) that stimulate keratinocyte hyperproliferation, leading to epidermal thickening (acanthosis). This distinguishes it from conditions primarily driven by growth factors (like some tumors), impaired apoptosis (like viral infections sometimes), reduced turnover (like aging skin), or pigmentary changes (like melasma). Remember the key histological features: acanthosis, parakeratosis, and Munro's microabscesses.

<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: Skin Pathology: Focus 03-43 (increased proliferation and turnover of epidermal k)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the histopathological features and underlying mechanisms of psoriasis, specifically focusing on the characteristic epidermal changes (acanthosis, parakeratosis, Munro's microabscesses) and the role of inflammatory mediators in driving epidermal hyperproliferation. (考察银屑病组织病理学特征，特别是表皮的变化（棘层增生、角化不全、Munro微脓肿）以及炎症介导物在驱动表皮过度增殖中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to the dermatology clinic with a chronic, relapsing-remitting skin condition characterized by well-demarcated, erythematous plaques covered with silvery scales, primarily affecting the extensor surfaces (elbows, knees) and scalp. He reports occasional flares triggered by stress and upper respiratory infections. He denies any significant family history of similar skin conditions or other systemic symptoms. A skin biopsy is performed from an active lesion on his elbow. Histopathological examination reveals marked epidermal thickening (acanthosis) with elongated rete ridges, thinning of the suprapapillary epidermis, dilated and tortuous capillaries in the dermal papillae, and collections of neutrophils within the stratum corneum (Munro's microabscesses). The epidermis shows incomplete keratinization (parakeratosis). Which of the following cellular processes is most directly responsible for the epidermal hyperproliferation observed in this patient?

(A) Increased activity of epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR) signaling pathway.
(B) Decreased expression of p53 tumor suppressor protein in keratinocytes.
(C) Enhanced production of interleukin-22 (IL-22) by activated T cells in the dermis.
(D) Reduced levels of keratinocyte differentiation markers, such as involucrin.
(E) Increased expression of cyclooxygenase-2 (COX-2) leading to prostaglandin synthesis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old male with chronic, relapsing-remitting skin condition. This chronicity and pattern are typical for psoriasis.
- **Clinical Features:** Well-demarcated, erythematous plaques with silvery scales on extensor surfaces (elbows, knees) and scalp. This is the classic presentation of plaque psoriasis.
- **Triggers:** Stress and upper respiratory infections are known triggers for psoriasis flares.
- **Histopathology:**
    - Marked epidermal thickening (acanthosis) with elongated rete ridges: Characteristic feature of psoriasis.
    - Thinning of the suprapapillary epidermis: Also typical.
    - Dilated and tortuous capillaries in the dermal papillae: Contributes to erythema.
    - Collections of neutrophils within the stratum corneum (Munro's microabscesses): Pathognomonic for psoriasis.
    - Incomplete keratinization (parakeratosis): Another hallmark feature.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most direct* cellular process responsible for the epidermal hyperproliferation (acanthosis).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Psoriasis Pathogenesis:** Psoriasis is an immune-mediated inflammatory disease primarily driven by T cells (Th1, Th17, Th22) infiltrating the skin.
- **Inflammatory Mediators:** These activated T cells release various pro-inflammatory cytokines, including TNF-α, IL-17, IL-22, and IL-23.
- **Keratinocyte Response:** These cytokines act on keratinocytes, stimulating their proliferation and inhibiting their differentiation.
- **IL-22 Role:** IL-22 is particularly crucial for keratinocyte hyperproliferation and epidermal thickening (acanthosis) in psoriasis. It activates STAT3 signaling in keratinocytes, promoting cell cycle progression and inhibiting terminal differentiation. It also upregulates antimicrobial peptides.
- **Other Mediators:** While TNF-α and IL-17 also contribute to inflammation and keratinocyte activation, IL-22 is considered a key driver of the epidermal hyperplasia seen in psoriasis.
- **Option Analysis:**
    - (A) EGFR signaling is involved in normal epidermal proliferation and wound healing, but its dysregulation is more associated with certain cancers, not the primary driver of psoriasis hyperproliferation.
    - (B) p53 is a tumor suppressor; its decreased expression could contribute to uncontrolled proliferation, but it's not the specific mechanism highlighted in psoriasis pathogenesis driven by inflammatory cytokines.
    - (C) IL-22 directly stimulates keratinocyte proliferation and inhibits differentiation, explaining the acanthosis and parakeratosis seen histologically. This aligns perfectly with the known pathophysiology.
    - (D) Reduced differentiation markers (like involucrin) are a *consequence* of the altered keratinocyte maturation process driven by cytokines like IL-22, not the primary *cause* of hyperproliferation itself.
    - (E) COX-2 and prostaglandins contribute to inflammation and vasodilation (erythema) in psoriasis, but they are not the primary drivers of the epidermal hyperproliferation (acanthosis).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Increased activity of epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR) signaling pathway.** EGFR signaling is important for normal epidermal homeostasis and wound healing. While some studies suggest altered EGFR signaling in psoriasis, it is not considered the primary driver of the characteristic hyperproliferation compared to the effects of specific inflammatory cytokines like IL-22. Psoriasis is primarily an immune-mediated disease, not a primary EGFR-driven proliferative disorder.
- (B): **Decreased expression of p53 tumor suppressor protein in keratinocytes.** p53 is a critical regulator of cell cycle arrest and apoptosis. Its downregulation can lead to uncontrolled proliferation, as seen in many cancers. While p53 levels might be altered in chronic inflammatory conditions, it's not the specific, well-established primary mechanism driving the hyperproliferation in psoriasis, which is directly linked to cytokine signaling.
- (D): **Reduced levels of keratinocyte differentiation markers, such as involucrin.** Involucrin is a marker of keratinocyte terminal differentiation. In psoriasis, the maturation process is accelerated, leading to incomplete differentiation and parakeratosis. This means that while the *expression* of late differentiation markers might be reduced or altered *relative* to the accelerated turnover, the *primary* event driving the hyperproliferation is the stimulation of keratinocyte division, not a primary defect in differentiation markers themselves. Reduced differentiation is a consequence, not the root cause of the proliferation.
- (E): **Increased expression of cyclooxygenase-2 (COX-2) leading to prostaglandin synthesis.** COX-2 is upregulated in psoriatic lesions, contributing to inflammation, vasodilation (erythema), and potentially pruritus. Prostaglandins are inflammatory mediators. However, COX-2 activity primarily relates to the inflammatory aspects and vascular changes, not the direct stimulation of keratinocyte proliferation that causes acanthosis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Psoriasis                                     | EGFR Signaling Dysregulation (e.g., Cancer) | p53 Deficiency (e.g., Cancer) | Impaired Differentiation (e.g., Ichthyosis) | COX-2 Overexpression (e.g., Inflammation) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Driver**   | Immune-mediated inflammation (T cells, IL-22) | Aberrant receptor activation                | Loss of tumor suppression      | Genetic defects in differentiation genes | Inflammation, Prostaglandins              |
| **Key Cytokine**     | IL-22, IL-17, TNF-α                           | N/A (Receptor-driven)                       | N/A                             | N/A                                         | N/A                                       |
| **Epidermal Change** | Acanthosis, Parakeratosis, Munro's abscesses  | Variable, often dysplastic changes          | Variable, often dysplastic changes| Variable, often hyperkeratosis, scale      | Erythema, Edema                           |
| **Cellular Process** | Keratinocyte hyperproliferation & impaired differentiation | Uncontrolled proliferation                 | Uncontrolled proliferation     | Impaired keratinization                     | Inflammation, Vasodilation                |
| **Role in Psoriasis**| Central                                        | Minor/Secondary                             | Minor/Secondary                 | Consequence of hyperproliferation          | Contributes to inflammation/erythema      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Psoriasis is characterized by epidermal hyperproliferation (acanthosis) and impaired differentiation (parakeratosis), driven primarily by inflammatory cytokines released by activated T cells, particularly IL-22. Understanding the role of specific cytokines in driving keratinocyte behavior is crucial. Key differentials for hyperkeratotic scaling plaques include eczema, tinea corporis, and cutaneous T-cell lymphoma, each with distinct histopathology and underlying mechanisms.

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Skin Pathology: Focus 03-44 (Psoriasis)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the histopathological features and underlying immune mechanisms of psoriasis, differentiating it from other inflammatory skin conditions like eczema and lichen planus. (理解银屑病的组织病理学特征和潜在的免疫机制，并将其与湿疹和扁平苔藓等其他炎症性皮肤病区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to the dermatology clinic with a 3-month history of itchy, erythematous plaques on his extensor surfaces (elbows, knees) and scalp. He reports occasional flaring associated with upper respiratory infections. Physical examination reveals well-demarcated, raised, erythematous plaques covered with thick, silvery scales. Nail pitting is also observed. A skin biopsy is performed from the edge of a plaque on his elbow. Histopathological examination shows epidermal hyperplasia with elongated rete ridges (clubbing), parakeratosis (retention of nuclei in the stratum corneum), thinning of the suprapapillary epidermis, dilated and tortuous capillaries in the dermal papillae, and a neutrophil-rich inflammatory infiltrate in the dermis and epidermis (Munro's microabscesses). Which of the following findings is MOST characteristic of the histopathology described?

(A) Spongiosis and eosinophilic infiltrate
(B) Hyperkeratosis, acanthosis, and lymphocytic infiltrate
(C) Interface dermatitis with vacuolar alteration of the basal cell layer
(D) Thickened basement membrane and colloid bodies
(E) Acanthosis nigricans with papillomatosis

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male, typical age range for psoriasis onset.
- **Symptoms:** Itchy, erythematous plaques on extensor surfaces (elbows, knees) and scalp, classic distribution for plaque psoriasis. Flaring with URI suggests an immune trigger.
- **Physical Exam:** Well-demarcated, raised, erythematous plaques with thick, silvery scales (classic description of psoriatic plaques). Nail pitting is also a characteristic finding in psoriasis.
- **Histopathology:** This is the key diagnostic information.
    - Epidermal hyperplasia with elongated rete ridges (clubbing): Acanthosis (thickening of the epidermis) and regular elongation of rete ridges.
    - Parakeratosis: Retention of nuclei in the stratum corneum, indicating incomplete keratinization.
    - Thinning of the suprapapillary epidermis: Often seen above elongated rete ridges.
    - Dilated and tortuous capillaries in the dermal papillae: Contributes to the redness.
    - Neutrophil-rich inflammatory infiltrate: Characteristic of psoriasis, forming Munro's microabscesses (collections of neutrophils in the stratum corneum).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST characteristic histopathological finding among the options provided, based on the described features.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Psoriasis Pathophysiology:** Psoriasis is a chronic inflammatory skin disease driven by an overactive immune system, particularly involving T helper cells (Th1, Th17) and cytokines like TNF-α, IL-17, and IL-23. This leads to accelerated epidermal cell turnover (epidermal hyperplasia) and inflammation.
- **Histopathology of Psoriasis:** The key features described in the vignette are hallmarks of psoriasis:
    - **Acanthosis:** Thickening of the epidermis due to increased proliferation of keratinocytes.
    - **Parakeratosis:** Incomplete keratinization, where nuclei are retained in the stratum corneum.
    - **Elongated Rete Ridges (Clubbing):** Regular downward projections of the epidermis.
    - **Neutrophilic Infiltrate:** Accumulation of neutrophils in the epidermis (Munro's microabscesses) and dermis.
    - **Dilated Capillaries:** In the dermal papillae, contributing to erythema.
    - **Thinning of Suprapapillary Plate:** Above the elongated rete ridges.
- **Option Analysis:**
    - (A) Spongiosis (intercellular edema in the epidermis) and eosinophilic infiltrate are characteristic of eczematous dermatitis (e.g., atopic dermatitis, contact dermatitis).
    - (B) Hyperkeratosis (thickened stratum corneum), acanthosis (thickened epidermis), and lymphocytic infiltrate are general features of chronic inflammatory skin conditions, but the combination with parakeratosis and neutrophilic infiltrate strongly points towards psoriasis. While lymphocytes are present, the *neutrophilic* infiltrate is more specific. However, among the choices, this option captures the key features of epidermal thickening (acanthosis/hyperkeratosis) and inflammation.
    - (C) Interface dermatitis (inflammation at the dermoepidermal junction) with vacuolar alteration of the basal cell layer is characteristic of lichen planus.
    - (D) Thickened basement membrane and colloid bodies (hemorrhagic degeneration of basal cells) are characteristic of dermatitis herpetiformis.
    - (E) Acanthosis nigricans is characterized by velvety hyperpigmentation and thickening of the skin, typically in flexural areas, associated with insulin resistance. Acanthosis nigricans is a clinical term, not a specific histopathological pattern described here, and papillomatosis (wart-like projections) is not typical of psoriasis.

- **Conclusion:** The vignette describes classic histopathological features of psoriasis. Option (B) best summarizes the key epidermal changes (hyperkeratosis, acanthosis) and inflammatory component (lymphocytic infiltrate, although neutrophils are more specific, this option is the closest fit among the choices). The combination of acanthosis, parakeratosis, and neutrophilic infiltrate is the most definitive pattern for psoriasis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Spongiosis and eosinophilic infiltrate:** This describes eczematous dermatitis. Spongiosis is intercellular edema in the epidermis, and eosinophils are prominent inflammatory cells. Psoriasis typically lacks significant spongiosis and has a predominantly neutrophilic infiltrate, not eosinophilic. This is incorrect.
- (B): **Hyperkeratosis, acanthosis, and lymphocytic infiltrate:** Hyperkeratosis (thickened stratum corneum) and acanthosis (thickened epidermis) are present in psoriasis. While the infiltrate is primarily neutrophilic, lymphocytes are also present. This option captures the epidermal thickening characteristic of psoriasis better than other options, although it doesn't mention the key neutrophilic infiltrate or parakeratosis. It is the *most* characteristic among the choices provided, focusing on the epidermal changes.
- (C): **Interface dermatitis with vacuolar alteration of the basal cell layer:** This is the hallmark of lichen planus, where inflammation primarily affects the dermoepidermal junction, leading to damage and degeneration of basal keratinocytes (vacuolar alteration). Psoriasis primarily involves the epidermis and superficial dermis, not typically an interface pattern. This is incorrect.
- (D): **Thickened basement membrane and colloid bodies:** This describes dermatitis herpetiformis, an autoimmune blistering disease associated with celiac disease. Colloid bodies are remnants of degenerated basal cells within microabscesses at the tips of the dermal papillae. Psoriasis does not typically show colloid bodies or a thickened basement membrane in this manner. This is incorrect.
- (E): **Acanthosis nigricans with papillomatosis:** Acanthosis nigricans is a clinical finding (velvety hyperpigmentation) associated with insulin resistance, not a specific histopathological pattern described in the vignette. Papillomatosis (wart-like projections) is not characteristic of psoriasis. This is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Psoriasis                                  | Eczema (Dermatitis)                     | Lichen Planus                            | Dermatitis Herpetiformis                 | Acanthosis Nigricans (Clinical)         |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- |
| **Epidermal Changes**   | Acanthosis, Parakeratosis, Clubbing        | Spongiosis, Variable Acanthosis         | Hyperkeratosis, Atrophy, Interface       | Variable, Blistering                     | Hyperpigmentation, Velvety Texture      |
| **Infiltrate**          | Neutrophilic (Munro's microabscesses)      | Eosinophilic, Lymphocytic               | Lymphocytic (Band-like at DEJ)           | Neutrophilic (Subepidermal, Microabscesses) | None specific                           |
| **Dermal Changes**      | Dilated Capillaries                        | Variable                                | Variable                                 | Variable                                 | None specific                           |
| **Key Histology**       | Munro's microabscesses, Neutrophils        | Spongiosis, Eosinophils                  | Interface dermatitis, Vacuolar alteration | Subepidermal blisters, IgA deposits      | Clinical finding, not specific histology |
| **Clinical Appearance** | Silvery scales, Extensor surfaces, Nail pits | Erythema, Vesicles, Pruritus, Flexural   | Purple, Polygonal papules, Wickham's striae | Vesicles, Pruritus, Symmetric distribution | Velvety, Hyperpigmented, Flexural       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Psoriasis is characterized histologically by epidermal hyperplasia (acanthosis), parakeratosis, elongated rete ridges, dilated capillaries, and a neutrophilic infiltrate (Munro's microabscesses). Differentiating it from eczema (spongiosis, eosinophils) and lichen planus (interface dermatitis, lymphocytic infiltrate) is crucial. Remember the key features: neutrophils, parakeratosis, and regular acanthosis/clubbing.

<a id='question-145'></a>

### Question 145: Skin Pathology: Focus 03-45 (of involvement include the knees, elbows,and scalp)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the clinical presentation, histopathology, and underlying pathophysiology of Psoriasis, specifically its characteristic features and differential diagnosis with other dermatological conditions. (考察银屑病的临床表现、组织病理学和潜在病理生理机制，以及与其他皮肤病的鉴别诊断。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to the dermatology clinic complaining of itchy, red, scaly plaques on his extensor surfaces, particularly his knees and elbows. He also reports similar lesions on his scalp. The plaques are well-demarcated and covered with thick, silvery scales. He denies any recent infections, medication changes, or significant stress. Family history is positive for psoriasis in his father. Physical examination reveals erythematous plaques with adherent silvery scales on the extensor surfaces of the knees and elbows, and scaling patches on the scalp. Nail pitting is also observed on several fingers. A skin biopsy is performed from a lesion on the elbow. Histopathology shows epidermal hyperplasia with elongated rete ridges (clubbing), parakeratosis, Munro's microabscesses (collections of neutrophils in the stratum corneum), and dilated, tortuous capillaries in the dermal papillae. Which of the following is the MOST likely underlying pathophysiological mechanism contributing to the observed clinical and histological findings in this patient?

(A) Deposition of immune complexes containing IgG and C3 in the dermal-epidermal junction, leading to complement activation and inflammation.
(B) Activation of keratinocytes by cytokines like TNF-α and IL-17, leading to accelerated epidermal turnover and inflammation.
(C) Autoantibodies targeting desmosomal proteins (desmoglein 3 and desmoglein 1), causing acantholysis and intraepidermal blistering.
(D) Granulomatous inflammation in the dermis with non-caseating granulomas composed of macrophages, epithelioid histiocytes, and giant cells.
(E) Degeneration of the basal cell layer with vacuolar alteration and apoptotic keratinocytes, leading to epidermal detachment and blister formation.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male. Age is consistent with typical onset of psoriasis.
- **Chief Complaint:** Itchy, red, scaly plaques. Classic description of psoriatic lesions.
- **Location:** Extensor surfaces (knees, elbows), scalp. Characteristic distribution for psoriasis (plaque psoriasis).
- **Appearance:** Well-demarcated, erythematous plaques with thick, silvery scales. Hallmark features of plaque psoriasis.
- **Family History:** Positive for psoriasis. Psoriasis has a strong genetic component.
- **Physical Exam:** Confirms plaques on extensor surfaces and scalp, nail pitting (another common feature of psoriasis).
- **Histopathology:** Epidermal hyperplasia (thickening), elongated rete ridges (clubbing), parakeratosis (nuclei retained in stratum corneum), Munro's microabscesses (neutrophils in stratum corneum), dilated capillaries in dermal papillae. These are pathognomonic histological features of psoriasis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely underlying pathophysiological mechanism.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- Psoriasis is a chronic inflammatory skin disease characterized by accelerated epidermal cell turnover.
- The pathogenesis involves a complex interplay between genetic predisposition, environmental triggers, and immune dysregulation.
- Key players in the immune response include T cells (especially Th1 and Th17 cells), dendritic cells, and keratinocytes.
- Activated T cells release pro-inflammatory cytokines, such as TNF-α (Tumor Necrosis Factor-alpha), IL-17 (Interleukin-17), IL-22, and IL-23.
- These cytokines act on keratinocytes, stimulating their proliferation and differentiation, leading to epidermal hyperplasia (thickening) and shortened transit time (accelerated turnover).
- IL-17 and TNF-α are particularly important in driving keratinocyte hyperproliferation and inflammation.
- The clinical features (redness, scaling) and histological findings (epidermal hyperplasia, parakeratosis, Munro's microabscesses) are direct consequences of this cytokine-mediated inflammation and accelerated epidermal turnover.
- Option (B) accurately describes this central mechanism: activation of keratinocytes by cytokines like TNF-α and IL-17, leading to accelerated epidermal turnover and inflammation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deposition of immune complexes containing IgG and C3 in the dermal-epidermal junction, leading to complement activation and inflammation. This describes the mechanism of **Lupus Erythematosus (SLE)**, specifically cutaneous lupus. While SLE can cause skin lesions, the clinical presentation (malar rash, discoid lesions) and histopathology (interface dermatitis, apoptotic keratinocytes) are different from psoriasis.
- (C): Autoantibodies targeting desmosomal proteins (desmoglein 3 and desmoglein 1), causing acantholysis and intraepidermal blistering. This describes the mechanism of **Pemphigus Vulgaris** (targeting desmoglein 3) and **Pemphigus Foliaceus** (targeting desmoglein 1). These are autoimmune blistering diseases, clinically and histologically distinct from psoriasis (blisters, acantholysis).
- (D): Granulomatous inflammation in the dermis with non-caseating granulomas composed of macrophages, epithelioid histiocytes, and giant cells. This describes the histopathology of **Granuloma Annulare** or **Sarcoidosis** affecting the skin. The clinical presentation (annular lesions) and histological features (granulomas) are different from psoriasis.
- (E): Degeneration of the basal cell layer with vacuolar alteration and apoptotic keratinocytes, leading to epidermal detachment and blister formation. This describes the mechanism of **Pemphigus Herpetiformis** or **Bullous Pemphigoid**, characterized by subepidermal blistering due to autoimmune attack on hemidesmosomes (BP) or basement membrane zone (PH). The clinical presentation (tense blisters) and histopathology (subepidermal split, eosinophils) are distinct from psoriasis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Psoriasis (Correct Answer)                                  | Lupus Erythematosus (A)                 | Pemphigus Vulgaris (C)                 | Granuloma Annulare (D)              | Pemphigus Herpetiformis/BP (E)       |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|
| **Clinical**        | Erythematous plaques, silvery scales, extensor surfaces, nails | Malar rash, discoid lesions, photosensitivity | Intraepidermal blisters, mucosal involvement | Annular lesions                      | Subepidermal blisters (tense/itchy)   |
| **Histopathology**  | Acanthosis, clubbing, parakeratosis, Munro's microabscesses | Interface dermatitis, apoptotic keratinocytes | Acantholysis, intraepidermal blisters   | Granulomas in dermis                 | Subepidermal split, eosinophils       |
| **Pathophysiology** | T-cell mediated, TNF-α, IL-17, keratinocyte hyperproliferation | Immune complex deposition, complement activation | Anti-desmoglein autoantibodies          | Unknown, possibly immune-mediated     | Anti-hemidesmosome/BMZ autoantibodies |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Psoriasis is characterized by T-cell mediated inflammation leading to keratinocyte hyperproliferation driven by cytokines like TNF-α and IL-17. This results in epidermal thickening (acanthosis), elongated rete ridges, parakeratosis, and Munro's microabscesses. Key differentials include other inflammatory skin conditions like lupus, blistering diseases like pemphigus and pemphigoid, and granulomatous disorders. Remember the classic presentation (plaques, scales, extensor surfaces, nails) and pathognomonic histology.

<a id='question-146'></a>

### Question 146: Skin Pathology: Focus 03-46 (well demarcated erythematous plaque with a silvery scale)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the recognition of the clinical presentation and histopathological features of psoriasis, specifically differentiating it from other dermatological conditions with similar appearances, and understanding the underlying immune dysregulation. (考察银屑病 (Psoriasis) 的临床表现和组织病理学特征，并与其他类似皮肤病的鉴别诊断，以及理解其潜在的免疫失调。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male presents to the dermatology clinic complaining of itchy, red patches on his elbows and knees that have been worsening over the past few months. He reports a family history of similar skin problems. Physical examination reveals well-demarcated, erythematous plaques covered with thick, silvery scales on the extensor surfaces of his elbows and knees. Nail pitting is also observed on several fingers. A skin biopsy is performed from one of the plaques. Histopathological examination shows epidermal hyperplasia with elongated rete ridges (clubbing), parakeratosis (retention of nuclei in the stratum corneum), Munro's microabscesses (collections of neutrophils in the stratum corneum), and dilated, tortuous capillaries in the dermal papillae. Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological mechanism contributing to this patient's condition?

(A) Deposition of immune complexes in the dermal-epidermal junction, leading to complement activation and inflammation.
(B) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against keratinocytes, resulting in epidermal cell death and blistering.
(C) Activation of Langerhans cells and subsequent release of pro-inflammatory cytokines, leading to keratinocyte hyperproliferation and inflammation.
(D) Autoantibodies targeting desmosomal proteins, causing acantholysis and intraepidermal blister formation.
(E) Mast cell degranulation triggered by IgE binding to allergens, leading to histamine release and urticarial lesions.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male, suggesting adult-onset psoriasis.
- **Symptoms:** Itchy, red patches on elbows and knees (classic extensor surfaces), worsening over months (chronic nature).
- **Family History:** Suggests a genetic predisposition, common in psoriasis.
- **Physical Exam:** Well-demarcated, erythematous plaques with thick, silvery scales on elbows and knees (hallmark presentation of plaque psoriasis). Nail pitting (another characteristic feature of psoriasis).
- **Histopathology:** Epidermal hyperplasia (thickening), elongated rete ridges (clubbing), parakeratosis (nuclei in stratum corneum), Munro's microabscesses (neutrophils in stratum corneum), dilated capillaries in dermal papillae (Auspitz sign clinically). These are classic histopathological findings of psoriasis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying immunological mechanism.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- Psoriasis is a chronic inflammatory skin disease characterized by epidermal hyperproliferation and inflammation.
- The pathogenesis involves a complex interplay between genetic predisposition and environmental triggers, leading to immune dysregulation.
- Key immune cells involved include T lymphocytes (especially Th1 and Th17 cells), dendritic cells (Langerhans cells), and neutrophils.
- Langerhans cells (dendritic cells in the epidermis) capture antigens and migrate to lymph nodes, where they present antigens to T cells.
- Activated T cells (Th1 and Th17) migrate back to the skin and release pro-inflammatory cytokines such as TNF-α, IL-17, IL-22, and IFN-γ.
- These cytokines stimulate keratinocyte hyperproliferation, leading to epidermal thickening (acanthosis) and abnormal differentiation (parakeratosis).
- IL-17 and TNF-α also promote inflammation by recruiting neutrophils, which form Munro's microabscesses.
- IL-22 contributes to epidermal hyperplasia.
- The dilated capillaries in the dermal papillae are a consequence of the inflammatory response.
- Therefore, the underlying mechanism involves activation of Langerhans cells and subsequent release of pro-inflammatory cytokines driving keratinocyte hyperproliferation and inflammation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deposition of immune complexes in the dermal-epidermal junction, leading to complement activation and inflammation. This describes the mechanism of **bullous pemphigoid** or **lupus erythematosus**, characterized by subepidermal blistering or interface dermatitis, respectively, not the epidermal hyperplasia and parakeratosis seen in psoriasis.
- (B): T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against keratinocytes, resulting in epidermal cell death and blistering. This describes the mechanism of **pemphigus vulgaris** (autoantibodies against desmosomes cause acantholysis and blistering) or **lichen planus** (T-cell mediated attack on basal keratinocytes). While T cells are involved in psoriasis, the primary mechanism is cytokine release leading to hyperproliferation, not direct cytotoxicity causing blistering.
- (D): Autoantibodies targeting desmosomal proteins, causing acantholysis and intraepidermal blister formation. This is the hallmark of **pemphigus vulgaris**, which presents with flaccid blisters and erosions, distinct from the plaques and scales of psoriasis.
- (E): Mast cell degranulation triggered by IgE binding to allergens, leading to histamine release and urticarial lesions. This describes the mechanism of **urticaria (hives)**, an allergic reaction characterized by transient wheals and flares, not the chronic plaques of psoriasis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Psoriasis                                  | Bullous Pemphigoid                       | Pemphigus Vulgaris                      | Urticaria                               |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|
| **Clinical**        | Erythematous plaques, silvery scales, nail pitting | Tense bullae on erythematous base        | Flaccid blisters, erosions                | Wheals, flares, pruritus                 |
| **Location**         | Extensor surfaces (elbows, knees), scalp, nails | Flexural areas, trunk                    | Mucosa, skin                             | Anywhere                                 |
| **Histopathology**   | Acanthosis, parakeratosis, Munro's abscesses, clubbing | Subepidermal blister, eosinophils        | Intraepidermal blister, acantholysis      | Perivascular edema, mast cells           |
| **Immunology**       | T-cell (Th1, Th17) mediated, cytokine release | IgG autoantibodies vs. BP180/BP230      | IgG autoantibodies vs. desmogleins       | IgE mediated mast cell degranulation     |
| **Key Cytokines**    | TNF-α, IL-17, IL-22, IFN-γ                 | None specific                             | None specific                             | Histamine                                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Psoriasis is an immune-mediated inflammatory disease characterized by epidermal hyperproliferation driven by T-cell activation and cytokine release (especially IL-17, TNF-α, IL-22). Key clinical features include well-demarcated erythematous plaques with silvery scales on extensor surfaces and nail changes. Histopathology shows acanthosis, parakeratosis, Munro's microabscesses, and dilated capillaries. Differentiate from bullous pemphigoid (subepidermal bullae, anti-BP antibodies), pemphigus vulgaris (intraepidermal bullae, anti-desmoglein antibodies), and urticaria (wheals, IgE mediated).

<a id='question-147'></a>

### Question 147: Skin Pathology: Focus 03-47 (in pinpoint bleeding (Auspitz sign). Nail beds sho)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the histopathological features and clinical presentation of psoriasis, specifically focusing on the characteristic epidermal changes, inflammatory infiltrate, and associated clinical signs like the Auspitz sign and nail changes. (本题考察银屑病的组织病理学特征和临床表现，特别是表皮变化、炎症浸润以及奥斯皮茨征和指甲改变等特征性临床体征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to the dermatology clinic with a chronic, relapsing skin condition. He reports itchy, erythematous plaques primarily on his extensor surfaces (elbows, knees) and scalp. Physical examination reveals well-demarcated, raised plaques covered with thick, silvery scales. Upon removal of the scales, pinpoint bleeding occurs (Auspitz sign). He also notes pitting and onycholysis (separation of the nail plate from the nail bed) in several fingernails. A skin biopsy is performed from an active lesion. Histopathology shows epidermal hyperplasia with elongated rete ridges (clubbing), parakeratosis (retention of nuclei in the stratum corneum), thinning of the suprapapillary epidermis, dilated and tortuous capillaries in the dermal papillae, and a perivascular inflammatory infiltrate composed mainly of neutrophils. Which of the following histopathological findings is MOST characteristic of this patient's condition?

(A) Spongiosis and intraepidermal vesicles
(B) Acanthosis nigricans with papillomatosis
(C) Hyperkeratosis, parakeratosis, and Munro's microabscesses
(D) Interface dermatitis with vacuolar alteration of the basal cell layer
(E) Granulomatous inflammation with non-caseating granulomas

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male with a chronic, relapsing skin condition. This suggests a chronic inflammatory dermatosis.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Itchy, erythematous plaques on extensor surfaces (elbows, knees) and scalp. This is a classic distribution for psoriasis.
- **Physical Exam Findings:** Well-demarcated, raised plaques with thick, silvery scales. This is the hallmark appearance of plaque psoriasis.
- **Auspitz Sign:** Pinpoint bleeding upon removal of scales. This is a characteristic, though not pathognomonic, sign of psoriasis due to the dilated, tortuous capillaries in the dermal papillae.
- **Nail Changes:** Pitting and onycholysis. These are common nail findings in psoriasis.
- **Histopathology:** Epidermal hyperplasia (thickening), elongated rete ridges (clubbing), parakeratosis (nuclei retained in stratum corneum), thinning of suprapapillary epidermis, dilated/tortuous capillaries, perivascular neutrophil infiltrate. These are the classic histological features of psoriasis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST characteristic histopathological finding.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Psoriasis Pathophysiology:** Psoriasis is a chronic immune-mediated inflammatory disease primarily affecting the skin. It involves dysregulation of the innate and adaptive immune systems, leading to keratinocyte hyperproliferation and inflammation. Key cytokines involved include TNF-α, IL-17, IL-22, and IL-23.
- **Histopathology of Psoriasis:**
    - **Epidermis:** Marked acanthosis (thickening) with elongated, club-shaped rete ridges extending deep into the dermis. Parakeratosis (retention of nuclei in the stratum corneum) is prominent. The suprapapillary epidermis is often thinned or absent.
    - **Dermis:** Dilated and tortuous capillaries in the dermal papillae contribute to the Auspitz sign. A perivascular inflammatory infiltrate, predominantly neutrophils (especially in acute lesions) and T lymphocytes, is present.
    - **Munro's Microabscesses:** Collections of neutrophils within the stratum corneum, particularly over the tips of the rete ridges, are characteristic but not always present.
    - **Koilocytes:** Dyskeratotic keratinocytes may be seen.
- **Clinical-Pathological Correlation:** The clinical findings (plaques, scales, Auspitz sign, nail changes) directly correlate with the histopathological features (acanthosis, parakeratosis, dilated capillaries, neutrophil infiltrate).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Spongiosis and intraepidermal vesicles:** This describes eczematous dermatitis (e.g., allergic contact dermatitis, atopic dermatitis). Spongiosis is intercellular edema in the epidermis, leading to vesicle formation. This is not characteristic of psoriasis.
- (B) **Acanthosis nigricans with papillomatosis:** Acanthosis nigricans is characterized by epidermal thickening (acanthosis) and hyperpigmentation, typically in flexural areas (neck, axillae, groin), often associated with insulin resistance or malignancy. Papillomatosis refers to nipple-like projections of the epidermis. This is unrelated to psoriasis.
- (D) **Interface dermatitis with vacuolar alteration of the basal cell layer:** This pattern is characteristic of lichen planus and other interface dermatoses, where inflammation primarily affects the dermoepidermal junction, leading to damage to basal keratinocytes. This is distinct from the primarily epidermal proliferation seen in psoriasis.
- (E) **Granulomatous inflammation with non-caseating granulomas:** This is characteristic of granulomatous diseases like sarcoidosis, granuloma annulare, or certain infections (e.g., tuberculosis). Granulomas are collections of macrophages, often with lymphocytes and giant cells. This pattern is not seen in psoriasis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Psoriasis                                  | Eczema (Dermatitis)                     | Lichen Planus                          | Acanthosis Nigricans                   | Sarcoidosis                             |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- |
| **Epidermal Changes**   | Acanthosis, clubbing, parakeratosis, thinning suprapapillary epidermis | Spongiosis, intraepidermal vesicles     | Interface dermatitis, basal vacuolization | Acanthosis, hyperpigmentation, papillomatosis | Variable, may show epidermal changes |
| **Inflammatory Infiltrate** | Perivascular neutrophils, T cells          | Perivascular lymphocytes, eosinophils   | Band-like lymphocytic infiltrate at DEJ | Variable                                | Non-caseating granulomas               |
| **Key Histology**       | Munro's microabscesses, dilated capillaries | Spongiosis                              | Sawtooth rete ridges, colloid bodies     | -                                       | Granulomas                              |
| **Clinical Appearance** | Silvery scales, plaques, Auspitz sign, nail pitting | Erythema, vesicles, scaling, excoriations | Purple, polygonal papules, Wickham's striae | Hyperpigmented, velvety plaques         | Variable, skin nodules, plaques         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Psoriasis is characterized by epidermal hyperplasia (acanthosis with clubbing), parakeratosis, thinning of the suprapapillary epidermis, dilated dermal capillaries, and a neutrophilic infiltrate. Munro's microabscesses (neutrophils in stratum corneum) are also characteristic. Differentiate from eczema (spongiosis), lichen planus (interface dermatitis), acanthosis nigricans (hyperpigmentation, flexural), and granulomatous diseases (granulomas).

<a id='question-148'></a>

### Question 148: Skin Pathology: Focus 03-48 (Psoriasis may be associated with arthritis,enterop)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Psoriasis is a chronic inflammatory skin disease characterized by epidermal hyperplasia, parakeratosis, and inflammatory cell infiltration, often associated with systemic manifestations like psoriatic arthritis and enteropathy. The key histological features include Munro's microabscesses (neutrophils in the stratum corneum) and acanthosis (epidermal thickening).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to the dermatology clinic with a 3-year history of recurrent, well-demarcated, erythematous plaques covered with silvery scales on his extensor surfaces (elbows, knees) and scalp. He reports intermittent joint pain and stiffness, primarily affecting his lower back and peripheral joints, which waxes and wanes. He denies any significant family history of skin or joint disease. A skin biopsy is performed on an active psoriatic plaque. Histopathological examination reveals marked epidermal hyperplasia (acanthosis) with elongated rete ridges, parakeratosis (retention of nuclei in the stratum corneum), thinning of the suprapapillary epidermis, dilated and tortuous capillaries in the dermal papillae, and collections of neutrophils within the stratum corneum. Immunohistochemical staining shows increased expression of TNF-alpha in the epidermis and dermis. Which of the following is the MOST likely associated systemic manifestation suggested by the patient's presentation and the histological findings?

(A) Crohn's disease
(B) Ulcerative colitis
(C) Rheumatoid arthritis
(D) Ankylosing spondylitis
(E) Celiac disease

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old male with classic psoriatic skin lesions (erythematous plaques, silvery scales on extensor surfaces, scalp). This strongly suggests psoriasis.
- **Associated Symptoms:** Intermittent joint pain and stiffness affecting the lower back and peripheral joints, waxing and waning pattern. This points towards psoriatic arthritis (PsA), a common comorbidity of psoriasis.
- **Histopathology:** Key features described are hallmarks of psoriasis: acanthosis (epidermal thickening), parakeratosis (nuclei in stratum corneum), Munro's microabscesses (neutrophils in stratum corneum), dilated capillaries in dermal papillae.
- **Immunohistochemistry:** Increased TNF-alpha expression is consistent with the inflammatory nature of psoriasis and PsA.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely associated systemic manifestation. The combination of psoriasis and inflammatory back/peripheral joint pain strongly suggests a specific type of spondyloarthropathy.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Psoriasis Pathophysiology:** Chronic immune-mediated inflammatory disease involving T cells (Th1, Th17), dendritic cells, and keratinocytes. Cytokines like TNF-alpha, IL-17, and IL-23 play crucial roles. This leads to accelerated epidermal turnover (hyperplasia, parakeratosis) and inflammation.
- **Psoriatic Arthritis (PsA):** An inflammatory arthritis occurring in up to 30% of psoriasis patients. It is a type of spondyloarthropathy.
- **Spondyloarthropathies:** A group of inflammatory rheumatic diseases sharing common features, including HLA-B27 association, enthesitis (inflammation where tendons/ligaments attach to bone), sacroiliitis, and axial involvement (spine). Psoriatic arthritis is one of the five main seronegative spondyloarthropathies (the others being ankylosing spondylitis, reactive arthritis, enteropathic arthritis, and undifferentiated spondyloarthropathy).
- **Axial Involvement:** The patient's lower back pain and stiffness are characteristic of axial involvement, a hallmark feature of ankylosing spondylitis and often seen in PsA.
- **Enteropathy:** Psoriasis can be associated with inflammatory bowel disease (IBD), particularly Crohn's disease (enteropathic arthritis). However, the prominent axial joint symptoms (lower back) in this patient make ankylosing spondylitis or PsA with axial involvement more likely than IBD-associated arthritis alone.
- **Differential Diagnosis:**
    - **Ankylosing Spondylitis (AS):** Primarily affects the axial skeleton (sacroiliac joints, spine). While it can have peripheral arthritis, the classic presentation is inflammatory back pain. It is strongly associated with HLA-B27. PsA can mimic AS, especially with axial involvement.
    - **Reactive Arthritis:** Typically follows a GI (e.g., Salmonella, Shigella, Campylobacter) or GU (e.g., Chlamydia) infection. Often presents with the triad of arthritis, urethritis, and conjunctivitis.
    - **Enteropathic Arthritis:** Arthritis associated with IBD (Crohn's or Ulcerative Colitis). Can be peripheral or axial.
    - **Rheumatoid Arthritis (RA):** A systemic autoimmune disease primarily affecting synovial joints, typically symmetrically in the hands and feet. Usually positive for rheumatoid factor (RF) and anti-CCP antibodies. Less commonly associated with psoriasis.
    - **Crohn's Disease/Ulcerative Colitis:** Inflammatory bowel diseases. Can be associated with arthritis (enteropathic arthritis), but the primary presentation here is skin and joint disease.

- **Connecting the Dots:** The patient has classic psoriasis (skin findings, histology) and symptoms highly suggestive of psoriatic arthritis, particularly axial involvement (lower back pain/stiffness). Psoriatic arthritis is a type of spondyloarthropathy. Ankylosing spondylitis is the prototypical spondyloarthropathy characterized by axial inflammation. Given the axial symptoms described, ankylosing spondylitis is a very strong possibility, and PsA often presents similarly with axial involvement. While Crohn's disease can be associated with psoriasis and arthritis (enteropathic arthritis), the specific pattern of axial joint pain points more strongly towards AS or PsA with axial features. Among the choices, Ankylosing Spondylitis is the most fitting systemic manifestation associated with the described axial joint symptoms in the context of psoriasis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Crohn's disease: While psoriasis can be associated with Crohn's disease (enteropathic arthritis), the primary joint symptoms described (axial involvement - lower back pain/stiffness) are more characteristic of spondyloarthropathies like ankylosing spondylitis or psoriatic arthritis with axial features, rather than Crohn's disease itself. Crohn's disease is primarily a GI disorder.
- (B) Ulcerative colitis: Similar to Crohn's disease, ulcerative colitis can be associated with arthritis (enteropathic arthritis), but the axial joint symptoms are less typical for UC-associated arthritis compared to AS or PsA.
- (C) Rheumatoid arthritis: RA is a distinct autoimmune disease primarily affecting peripheral joints symmetrically. It is not typically associated with psoriasis, although both are inflammatory conditions. The joint pattern (axial involvement) and the presence of psoriasis make RA less likely.
- (E) Celiac disease: Celiac disease is an autoimmune disorder triggered by gluten, primarily affecting the small intestine. While it can have extraintestinal manifestations, including arthritis, it is not a common or characteristic association with psoriasis, especially compared to spondyloarthropathies.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Psoriasis + Axial Arthritis (PsA/AS) | Crohn's Disease (Enteropathic Arthritis) | Ulcerative Colitis (Enteropathic Arthritis) | Rheumatoid Arthritis | Celiac Disease |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|----------------------|----------------|
| **Primary Disease** | Psoriasis                             | Crohn's Disease                           | Ulcerative Colitis                        | Rheumatoid Arthritis | Celiac Disease |
| **Skin Findings**    | Psoriatic plaques                    | Erythema nodosum, Pyoderma gangrenosum    | Erythema nodosum, Pyoderma gangrenosum     | None specific        | Dermatitis Herpetiformis |
| **Joint Pattern**    | Axial (back), Peripheral, Enthesitis | Peripheral, Axial (less common)           | Peripheral, Axial (less common)            | Symmetrical Peripheral | Peripheral     |
| **HLA-B27**          | Often positive (especially axial)     | Not typically associated                 | Not typically associated                  | Not associated       | HLA-DQ2/DQ8    |
| **GI Symptoms**      | May have IBD (less common)            | Diarrhea, abdominal pain, weight loss     | Bloody diarrhea, abdominal pain            | None specific        | Malabsorption |
| **Key Association**  | Spondyloarthropathy                  | IBD                                      | IBD                                       | Autoimmune           | Gluten Intolerance |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Psoriasis is frequently associated with systemic manifestations, most notably psoriatic arthritis, which is a type of spondyloarthropathy. Axial involvement (lower back pain/stiffness) is a key feature of spondyloarthropathies like ankylosing spondylitis and psoriatic arthritis. Remember the association between psoriasis and spondyloarthropathies, particularly axial arthritis. Differentiate PsA/AS from IBD-associated arthritis and RA based on clinical presentation (joint pattern, skin findings).
