<a id='question-149'></a>

### Question 149: Skin Pathology: Focus 03-49 (© Richard P. Usatine, MD Used with permission.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathogenesis, clinical presentation, and histological features of **Bullous Pemphigoid (BP)**, specifically differentiating it from other blistering diseases like Pemphigus Vulgaris and distinguishing its characteristic immunofluorescence pattern. (本题考察大疱性天疱疮 (Bullous Pemphigoid, BP) 的发病机制、临床表现和组织学特征，特别是将其与其他大疱性疾病，如寻常性天疱疮 (Pemphigus Vulgaris) 区分开来，并识别其特征性免疫荧光模式。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 78-year-old woman presents to the dermatology clinic with a 3-month history of intensely pruritic, tense bullae primarily affecting her flexural areas (axillae, groin) and abdomen. She denies mucosal involvement. Her past medical history is significant for hypertension and osteoarthritis. Physical examination reveals multiple, large, tense, non-erosed bullae on an erythematous base, predominantly in the flexural regions. Nikolsky sign is negative. A skin biopsy is obtained from the base of a blister. Direct immunofluorescence (DIF) microscopy of the perilesional skin shows linear deposition of IgG and C3 along the basement membrane zone (BMZ). Indirect immunofluorescence (IIF) using monkey esophagus substrate shows circulating IgG autoantibodies binding to the BMZ.

What is the most likely underlying pathogenic mechanism responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Autoantibodies targeting desmoglein 3, leading to suprabasal acantholysis and intraepidermal blistering.
(B) Autoantibodies targeting desmoglein 1 and desmoglein 3, leading to suprabasal acantholysis and intraepidermal blistering, often involving mucous membranes.
(C) Autoantibodies targeting BP180 (collagen VII) and BP230 (plakophilin 1) components of the hemidesmosome, leading to subepidermal blistering.
(D) Deposition of immune complexes containing IgA in the dermal papillae, leading to subepidermal blistering and neutrophilic microabscesses.
(E) Autoantibodies targeting the nicotinic acetylcholine receptor at the neuromuscular junction, leading to muscle weakness and fatigability, not typically associated with blistering skin disease.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Demographics:** 78-year-old woman - Bullous Pemphigoid typically affects the elderly (>60 years).
- **Symptoms:** 3-month history of intensely pruritic, tense bullae - Pruritus is a hallmark symptom of BP. Tense bullae suggest a subepidermal split.
- **Location:** Flexural areas (axillae, groin) and abdomen - Common sites for BP.
- **Mucosal Involvement:** Denies mucosal involvement - This helps differentiate BP from Pemphigus Vulgaris, which commonly affects mucous membranes.
- **Physical Exam:** Multiple, large, tense, non-erosed bullae on an erythematous base. Nikolsky sign negative - Tense bullae and negative Nikolsky sign are characteristic of subepidermal blistering (like BP), whereas flaccid bullae and positive Nikolsky sign are seen in intraepidermal blistering (like Pemphigus Vulgaris).
- **Diagnostic Studies:**
    - **Skin Biopsy:** Base of a blister.
    - **Direct Immunofluorescence (DIF):** Linear deposition of IgG and C3 along the basement membrane zone (BMZ) - This is the pathognomonic finding for Bullous Pemphigoid.
    - **Indirect Immunofluorescence (IIF):** Circulating IgG autoantibodies binding to the BMZ - Confirms the presence of autoantibodies against BMZ components.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying pathogenic mechanism.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- The clinical presentation (elderly patient, pruritic tense bullae, flexural distribution, negative Nikolsky sign, no mucosal involvement) strongly suggests Bullous Pemphigoid.
- The key diagnostic findings are the DIF showing linear IgG and C3 deposition along the BMZ and the IIF showing circulating autoantibodies against the BMZ.
- Bullous Pemphigoid is an autoimmune disease where autoantibodies are directed against components of the hemidesmosome, which anchors the basal keratinocytes to the basement membrane.
- The two main target antigens are BP180 (also known as collagen XVII or type VII collagen) and BP230 (also known as plakophilin 1). BP180 is a transmembrane protein, while BP230 is an intracellular plaque protein.
- Binding of these autoantibodies to the hemidesmosome components triggers complement activation (leading to C3 deposition) and inflammation, ultimately causing separation at the basement membrane zone, resulting in subepidermal blistering.
- Therefore, the pathogenic mechanism involves autoantibodies against hemidesmosomal proteins (BP180 and BP230).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Autoantibodies targeting desmoglein 3 (Dsg3) cause Pemphigus Vulgaris. Dsg3 is a component of desmosomes, which connect keratinocytes within the epidermis. Antibodies against Dsg3 lead to loss of cell-cell adhesion (acantholysis) within the epidermis, resulting in intraepidermal blisters, typically suprabasal. Mucosal involvement is common. This mechanism does not fit the subepidermal blistering and BMZ immunofluorescence pattern seen in the vignette.
- (B): Autoantibodies targeting desmoglein 1 (Dsg1) and desmoglein 3 (Dsg3) cause Pemphigus Vulgaris. Antibodies against Dsg3 cause suprabasal acantholysis, while antibodies against Dsg1 cause more superficial acantholysis. Both lead to intraepidermal blisters. Mucosal involvement is very common. This mechanism does not fit the subepidermal blistering and BMZ immunofluorescence pattern seen in the vignette.
- (D): Deposition of IgA in the dermal papillae is characteristic of Dermatitis Herpetiformis (DH). DH presents with intensely pruritic papules and vesicles, often symmetrically distributed on extensor surfaces (elbows, knees, buttocks). Histology shows neutrophilic microabscesses in the dermal papillae. DIF shows granular IgA deposits in the dermal papillae. This mechanism and clinical presentation differ significantly from the vignette.
- (E): Autoantibodies targeting the nicotinic acetylcholine receptor (nAChR) at the neuromuscular junction cause Myasthenia Gravis (MG). MG is characterized by fluctuating muscle weakness and fatigability, affecting ocular, bulbar, limb, and respiratory muscles. It is not a primary skin blistering disease. While some patients with autoimmune blistering diseases can have associated neuromuscular disorders, this is not the primary mechanism of the skin findings described.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Bullous Pemphigoid (BP)                                  | Pemphigus Vulgaris (PV)                               | Dermatitis Herpetiformis (DH)                       | Myasthenia Gravis (MG)                               |
|----------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| **Target Antigen**   | BP180 (Collagen XVII), BP230 (Plakophilin 1)             | Desmoglein 3 (Dsg3), Desmoglein 1 (Dsg1)              | Tissue transglutaminase (tTG), Epidermal transglutaminase (eTG) | Nicotinic Acetylcholine Receptor (nAChR)             |
| **Location**         | Hemidesmosome (BMZ)                                      | Desmosome (Intraepidermal)                            | Dermal Papillae                                    | Neuromuscular Junction                               |
| **Blister Level**    | Subepidermal                                             | Intraepidermal (Suprabasal or Superficial)            | Subepidermal                                        | N/A (Muscle Weakness)                                |
| **Blister Type**     | Tense                                                    | Flaccid                                                | Vesicles/Papules                                    | N/A                                                  |
| **Nikolsky Sign**    | Negative                                                 | Positive                                               | Negative                                            | N/A                                                  |
| **Mucosal Involvement**| Rare                                                     | Common                                                 | Rare                                                | N/A                                                  |
| **Pruritus**         | Intense                                                  | Variable                                               | Intense                                             | N/A                                                  |
| **DIF Pattern**      | Linear IgG/C3 at BMZ                                      | Intercellular IgG/C3 (fishnet pattern)                 | Granular IgA in dermal papillae                     | N/A                                                  |
| **Clinical Presentation**| Elderly, tense bullae, flexural areas, pruritus          | Mucosal erosions, flaccid bullae, widespread          | Pruritic papules/vesicles, extensor surfaces        | Fluctuating muscle weakness, fatigability            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Bullous Pemphigoid is characterized by autoantibodies against hemidesmosomal proteins (BP180 and BP230), leading to subepidermal blistering. Key diagnostic features include tense bullae, negative Nikolsky sign, and linear IgG/C3 deposition at the basement membrane zone on DIF. Differentiate it from Pemphigus Vulgaris (intraepidermal blisters, positive Nikolsky, mucosal involvement, intercellular DIF) and Dermatitis Herpetiformis (IgA deposition in dermal papillae, neutrophilic microabscesses).

<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Skin Pathology: Focus 03-50 (• Microscopically,the lesions show epidermal hyper)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the histopathological features of Psoriasis, specifically epidermal hyperplasia (acanthosis), parakeratosis, Munro's microabscesses, and dilated capillaries in the dermal papillae, and differentiating it from other dermatological conditions with similar presentations.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to the dermatology clinic with a chronic, relapsing-remitting skin condition characterized by well-demarcated, erythematous plaques covered with silvery scales, primarily affecting the extensor surfaces (elbows, knees), scalp, and lower back. The patient reports significant pruritus and notes that the condition worsens during periods of stress. He denies any recent systemic illness, medication changes, or significant family history of similar skin conditions, although his mother had a similar rash. Physical examination reveals thick, adherent silvery scales upon scraping of which reveals pinpoint bleeding (Auspitz sign). A skin biopsy is performed from an active lesion on his elbow. Histopathological examination shows marked epidermal hyperplasia with elongated rete ridges (acanthosis), collections of neutrophils within the stratum corneum (parakeratosis with Munro's microabscesses), thinning of the suprapapillary epidermis, and dilated, tortuous capillaries in the dermal papillae. Which of the following histopathological findings is MOST characteristic of this patient's condition?

(A) Spongiosis and intraepidermal vesicles within the stratum spinosum.
(B) Thickened basement membrane and subepidermal bullae formation.
(C) Granulomatous inflammation with non-caseating granulomas in the dermis.
(D) Marked acanthosis, parakeratosis with Munro's microabscesses, and dilated dermal capillaries.
(E) Epidermal atrophy, vacuolar degeneration of the basal cell layer, and lymphocytic infiltrate in the dermis.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male, suggesting an adult onset.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Chronic, relapsing-remitting skin condition. Well-demarcated, erythematous plaques with silvery scales on extensor surfaces (elbows, knees), scalp, lower back. This is a classic description of plaque psoriasis.
- **Symptoms:** Pruritus, worsening with stress (common triggers for psoriasis).
- **Physical Exam:** Auspitz sign (pinpoint bleeding upon scale removal) is highly suggestive of psoriasis.
- **Family History:** Mother with similar rash suggests a genetic predisposition, common in psoriasis.
- **Histopathology:** Marked epidermal hyperplasia (acanthosis), elongated rete ridges, parakeratosis (nuclei in stratum corneum), Munro's microabscesses (neutrophils in stratum corneum), thinning of suprapapillary epidermis, dilated dermal capillaries. These are the hallmark histological features of psoriasis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST characteristic histopathological finding based on the clinical presentation and provided biopsy results.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Psoriasis Pathophysiology:** Psoriasis is a chronic inflammatory skin disease characterized by accelerated epidermal cell turnover, leading to epidermal hyperplasia (acanthosis). This is driven by dysregulation of the immune system, particularly involving T-helper cells (Th1, Th17) and cytokines like TNF-α, IL-17, and IL-23.
- **Histopathology:** The key features described in the vignette are:
    - **Acanthosis:** Thickening of the epidermis due to increased proliferation of keratinocytes, resulting in elongated rete ridges.
    - **Parakeratosis:** Retention of nuclei in the stratum corneum, indicating incomplete keratinization and rapid cell turnover.
    - **Munro's Microabscesses:** Collections of neutrophils within the stratum corneum, representing an influx of inflammatory cells.
    - **Thinning of Suprapapillary Epidermis:** Epidermal thinning directly above the dermal papillae.
    - **Dilated Dermal Capillaries:** Tortuous, dilated capillaries in the dermal papillae, contributing to the erythema (redness) of the plaques.
- **Option Analysis:** The question asks for the *most characteristic* finding. Option D perfectly encapsulates the classic histological triad of psoriasis: acanthosis, parakeratosis (often with Munro's microabscesses), and dilated dermal capillaries.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Spongiosis and intraepidermal vesicles within the stratum spinosum:** This describes eczematous dermatitis (e.g., allergic contact dermatitis, atopic dermatitis). Spongiosis is intercellular edema in the epidermis, leading to vesicle formation. This is not characteristic of psoriasis.
- (B) **Thickened basement membrane and subepidermal bullae formation:** This is characteristic of bullous pemphigoid, an autoimmune blistering disease targeting hemidesmosomes at the dermal-epidermal junction. Psoriasis does not typically involve subepidermal blistering.
- (C) **Granulomatous inflammation with non-caseating granulomas in the dermis:** This is characteristic of granulomatous skin conditions like granuloma annulare, sarcoidosis, or leprosy. Psoriasis is characterized by neutrophilic inflammation (Munro's microabscesses) and lymphocytic infiltrate, not granulomas.
- (E) **Epidermal atrophy, vacuolar degeneration of the basal cell layer, and lymphocytic infiltrate in the dermis:** This describes lichen planus. Lichen planus features a band-like lymphocytic infiltrate at the dermal-epidermal junction, basal cell vacuolization (liquefaction degeneration), and often epidermal thinning (atrophy). This is distinctly different from the hyperplastic epidermis seen in psoriasis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Psoriasis (Correct Answer)                                  | Eczema (Distractor A)                                  | Bullous Pemphigoid (Distractor B)                      | Granuloma Annulare (Distractor C)                     | Lichen Planus (Distractor E)                           |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Epidermis**           | Acanthosis (thickening), Parakeratosis, Munro's microabscesses | Spongiosis (intercellular edema), Intraepidermal vesicles | Usually normal thickness, may show acanthosis nigricans | Usually normal thickness                                | Atrophy (thinning), Vacuolar degeneration (basal cells) |
| **Dermal-Epidermal Junction** | Thinning of suprapapillary epidermis                      | Spongiosis                                              | Thickened basement membrane, Subepidermal bullae        | Usually normal                                          | Band-like lymphocytic infiltrate                       |
| **Dermis**              | Dilated capillaries, Neutrophilic infiltrate, Lymphocytes   | Lymphocytic infiltrate, Eosinophils (sometimes)         | Lymphocytic infiltrate, Eosinophils, Neutrophils        | Granulomas (non-caseating)                              | Granulomas (non-caseating)                              |
| **Key Histology**       | Acanthosis, Parakeratosis, Munro's microabscesses, Dilated capillaries | Spongiosis, Vesicles                                     | Subepidermal bullae                                     | Granulomas                                              | Band-like infiltrate, Vacuolar degeneration, Atrophy    |
| **Clinical Appearance** | Erythematous plaques, silvery scales, Auspitz sign           | Erythema, Vesicles, Crusting, Lichenification            | Tense bullae on erythematous base                       | Annular (ring-shaped) erythematous papules/plaques      | Violaceous, flat-topped papules, Wickham's striae        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Psoriasis is characterized by epidermal hyperplasia (acanthosis), incomplete keratinization (parakeratosis), neutrophilic collections (Munro's microabscesses), and dilated dermal capillaries. Differentiating it histologically from eczema (spongiosis), bullous pemphigoid (subepidermal bullae), granulomatous diseases (granulomas), and lichen planus (basal cell damage, band-like infiltrate) is crucial. Remember the classic triad for psoriasis histology.
